Cross-validation: evaluating estimator performancecite66†# L74: Learning the parameters of a prediction function and testing it on the same data is a methodological mistake: a model that would just repeat the labels of the samples that it has just seen would have a perfect score but would fail to predict anything useful on yet-unseen data. This situation is called overfitting. To avoid it, it is common practice when performing a (supervised) machine learning experiment to hold out part of the available data as a test set `X_test, y_test`.\nL75: Note that the word “experiment” is not intended to denote academic use only, because even in commercial settings machine learning usually starts out experimentally. Here is a flowchart of typical cross validation workflow in model training. The best parameters can be determined by cite67†grid search techniques.\nL76: In scikit-learn a random split into training and test sets can be quickly computed with the cite68†`train_test_split` helper function. Let’s load the iris data set to fit a linear support vector machine on it:\nL77: \nL78:     >>> import numpy as np\nL79:     >>> from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split\nL80:     >>> from sklearn import datasets\nL81:     >>> from sklearn import svm\nL82: \nL83:     >>> X, y = datasets.load_iris(return_X_y=True)\nL84:     >>> X.shape, y.shape\nL85:     ((150, 4), (150,))\nL86: We can now quickly sample a training set while holding out 40% of the data for testing (evaluating) our classifier:\nL87: \nL88:     >>> X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(\nL89:     ...     X, y, test_size=0.4, random_state=0)\nL90: \nL91:     >>> X_train.shape, y_train.shape\nL92:     ((90, 4), (90,))\nL93:     >>> X_test.shape, y_test.shape\nL94:     ((60, 4), (60,))\nL95: \nL96:     >>> clf = svm.SVC(kernel='linear', C=1).fit(X_train, y_train)\nL97:     >>> clf.score(X_test, y_test)\nL98:     0.96\nL99: When evaluating different settings (“hyperparameters”) for estimators, such as the `C` setting that must be manually set for an SVM, there is still a risk of overfitting on the test set because the parameters can be tweaked until the estimator performs optimally. This way, knowledge about the test set can “leak” into the model and evaluation metrics no longer report on generalization performance.\nL100: To solve this problem, yet another part of the dataset can be held out as a so-called “validation set”: training proceeds on the training set, after which evaluation is done on the validation set, and when the experiment seems to be successful, final evaluation can be done on the test set.\nL101: However, by partitioning the available data into three sets, we drastically reduce the number of samples which can be used for learning the model, and the results can depend on a particular random choice for the pair of (train, validation) sets.\nL102: A solution to this problem is a procedure called cite69†cross-validation†en.wikipedia.org (CV for short). A test set should still be held out for final evaluation, but the validation set is no longer needed when doing CV. In the basic approach, called k-fold CV, the training set is split into k smaller sets (other approaches are described below, but generally follow the same principles). The following procedure is followed for each of the k “folds”:\nL103:   * A model is trained using \\(k-1\\) of the folds as training data;\nL104: \nL105:   * the resulting model is validated on the remaining part of the data (i.e., it is used as a test set to compute a performance measure such as accuracy).\nL106: The performance measure reported by k-fold cross-validation is then the average of the values computed in the loop. This approach can be computationally expensive, but does not waste too much data (as is the case when fixing an arbitrary validation set), which is a major advantage in problems such as inverse inference where the number of samples is very small.\nL107: ## 3.1.1. Computing cross-validated metricscite70†# L108: \nL109: The simplest way to use cross-validation is to call the cite71†`cross_val_score` helper function on the estimator and the dataset.\nL110: The following example demonstrates how to estimate the accuracy of a linear kernel support vector machine on the iris dataset by splitting the data, fitting a model and computing the score 5 consecutive times (with different splits each time):\nL111: \nL112:     >>> from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score\nL113:     >>> clf = svm.SVC(kernel='linear', C=1, random_state=42)\nL114:     >>> scores = cross_val_score(clf, X, y, cv=5)\nL115:     >>> scores\nL116:     array([0.96, 1. , 0.96, 0.96, 1. ])\nL117: The mean score and the standard deviation are hence given by:\nL118: \nL119:     >>> print(\"%0.2f accuracy with a standard deviation of %0.2f\" % (scores.mean(), scores.std()))\nL120:     0.98 accuracy with a standard deviation of 0.02\nL121: By default, the score computed at each CV iteration is the `score` method of the estimator. It is possible to change this by using the scoring parameter:\nL122: \nL123:     >>> from sklearn import metrics\nL124:     >>> scores = cross_val_score(\nL125:     ...     clf, X, y, cv=5, scoring='f1_macro')\nL126:     >>> scores\nL127:     array([0.96, 1., 0.96, 0.96, 1.])\nL128: See cite72†The scoring parameter: defining model evaluation rules for details. In the case of the Iris dataset, the samples are balanced across target classes hence the accuracy and the F1-score are almost equal.\nL129: \nL130: When the `cv` argument is an integer, cite71†`cross_val_score` uses the cite73†`KFold` or cite74†`StratifiedKFold` strategies by default, the latter being used if the estimator derives from cite75†`ClassifierMixin` .\nL131: It is also possible to use other cross validation strategies by passing a cross validation iterator instead, for instance:\nL132: \nL133:     >>> from sklearn.model_selection import ShuffleSplit\nL134:     >>> n_samples = X.shape[0]\nL135:     >>> cv = ShuffleSplit(n_splits=5, test_size=0.3, random_state=0)\nL136:     >>> cross_val_score(clf, X, y, cv=cv)\nL137:     array([0.977, 0.977, 1., 0.955, 1.])\nL138: Another option is to use an iterable yielding (train, test) splits as arrays of indices, for example:\nL139: \nL140:     >>> def custom_cv_2folds(X):\nL141:     ...     n = X.shape[0]\nL142:     ...     i = 1\nL143:     ...     while i <= 2:\nL144:     ...         idx = np.arange(n * (i - 1) / 2, n * i / 2, dtype=int)\nL145:     ...         yield idx, idx\nL146:     ...         i += 1\nL147:     ...\nL148:     >>> custom_cv = custom_cv_2folds(X)\nL149:     >>> cross_val_score(clf, X, y, cv=custom_cv)\nL150:     array([1.        , 0.973])\nL151: Data transformation with held-out datacite76†# L152: Just as it is important to test a predictor on data held-out from training, preprocessing (such as standardization, feature selection, etc.) and similar cite77†data transformations similarly should be learnt from a training set and applied to held-out data for prediction:\nL153: \nL154:     >>> from sklearn import preprocessing\nL155:     >>> X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(\nL156:     ...     X, y, test_size=0.4, random_state=0)\nL157:     >>> scaler = preprocessing.StandardScaler().fit(X_train)\nL158:     >>> X_train_transformed = scaler.transform(X_train)\nL159:     >>> clf = svm.SVC(C=1).fit(X_train_transformed, y_train)\nL160:     >>> X_test_transformed = scaler.transform(X_test)\nL161:     >>> clf.score(X_test_transformed, y_test)\nL162:     0.9333\nL163: A cite78†`Pipeline` makes it easier to compose estimators, providing this behavior under cross-validation:\nL164: \nL165:     >>> from sklearn.pipeline import make_pipeline\nL166:     >>> clf = make_pipeline(preprocessing.StandardScaler(), svm.SVC(C=1))\nL167:     >>> cross_val_score(clf, X, y, cv=cv)\nL168:     array([0.977, 0.933, 0.955, 0.933, 0.977])\nL169: \nL170: \nL171: See cite79†Pipelines and composite estimators .\nL172: ### 3.1.1.1. The cross_validate function and multiple metric evaluationcite80†# L173: \nL174: The cite81†`cross_validate` function differs from cite71†`cross_val_score` in two ways:\nL175: \nL176:   * It allows specifying multiple metrics for evaluation.\nL177: \nL178:   * It returns a dict containing fit-times, score-times (and optionally training scores, fitted estimators, train-test split indices) in addition to the test score.\nL179: For single metric evaluation, where the scoring parameter is a string, callable or None, the keys will be - `['test_score', 'fit_time', 'score_time']`\nL180: \nL181: And for multiple metric evaluation, the return value is a dict with the following keys - `['test_<scorer1_name>', 'test_<scorer2_name>', 'test_<scorer...>', 'fit_time', 'score_time']`\nL182: `return_train_score` is set to `False` by default to save computation time. To evaluate the scores on the training set as well you need to set it to `True`. You may also retain the estimator fitted on each training set by setting `return_estimator=True`. Similarly, you may set `return_indices=True` to retain the training and testing indices used to split the dataset into train and test sets for each cv split.\nL183: The multiple metrics can be specified either as a list, tuple or set of predefined scorer names:\nL184: \nL185:     >>> from sklearn.model_selection import cross_validate\nL186:     >>> from sklearn.metrics import recall_score\nL187:     >>> scoring = ['precision_macro', 'recall_macro']\nL188:     >>> clf = svm.SVC(kernel='linear', C=1, random_state=0)\nL189:     >>> scores = cross_validate(clf, X, y, scoring=scoring)\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n5.2. Permutation feature importance — scikit-learn 1.9.1 documentation (https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/permutation_importance.html)\nciteturn37view1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today; Content type: text/html; Source: open({\"ref_id\":\"https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/permutation_importance.html\",\"lineno\":null}); Total lines: 203\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n12. Las tareas que deben mantenerse separadas\\r\\n\\r\\n| Tarea | Entrada y salida | Relación con nuestro objetivo |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Reconstrucción espacial | Presiones disponibles en t → presiones no medidas en t | Comparte variables, pero no anticipa t+15. |\\r\\n| Emulación hidráulica | Demandas/condiciones de una simulación → presión de esa condición | Solo sería forecast si todas las condiciones futuras fueran conocidas al emitirlo. |\\r\\n| Pronóstico temporal | Lecturas hasta t → presión en t+h | Antecedente directo; deben verificarse h y la disponibilidad de X. |\\r\\n| Referencia saludable | Lecturas/modelo → presión esperada sin una fuga nueva | Se optimiza para producir residuos diagnósticos, no necesariamente para seguir la presión real durante una fuga. |\\r\\n| Detección/localización | Señales o residuos → alarma, tubería o zona | Su score económico/recall no mide MAE de forecast. |\\r\\n| Reconstrucción de una secuencia | Secuencia → esa misma secuencia reconstruida | Un LSTM-autoencoder no se convierte en predictor futuro por usar memoria temporal. |\\r\\n\\r\\nEsta separación explica buena parte de las aparentes contradicciones entre artículos. El estado del sensor objetivo actual puede mantenerse en todas las comparaciones, y se declara como sensor obligatorio. Después puede estudiarse por separado su ausencia; no confundir ese caso con ausencia de sensores auxiliares.\\r\\n\\r\\nSVR es una alternativa del curso, pero sobre más de 100.000 filas su costo puede ser alto; si se utiliza muestreo de entrenamiento, mantener el mismo test e informar exactamente cómo se seleccionó. GNN puede quedar como ampliación si el tiempo permite reproducir una referencia.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Partición temporal y evaluación\\r\\n\\r\\nPropuesta a congelar antes de entrenar:\\r\\n\\r\\n- Entrenamiento inicial enero–agosto 2018; validación septiembre–octubre 2018; calibración noviembre–diciembre 2018 si se añaden intervalos; prueba final 2019.\\r\\n- Para tuning, folds cronológicos expansivos dentro del período de desarrollo. No k-fold con barajado. Las fechas son propuesta, no partición ya ejecutada.\\r\\n- Asignar ejemplos por tiempo de **etiqueta**: un origen de diciembre cuyo target cae en enero no entra al entrenamiento 2018. Purgar los últimos h orígenes al cruzar un límite, o imponer directamente `target_time <= train_end`.\\r\\n- Permitir historia pasada para producir el primer pronóstico de prueba, si se declara como contexto operacional observado. El solapamiento de historia no justifica usar futuras etiquetas; una evaluación adicional con ventanas totalmente separadas puede estudiar sensibilidad.\\r\\n- Cada modificación tras observar el test convierte ese conjunto en desarrollo. El test 2019 se usa una sola vez una vez congelados sensor, familia, rezagos, hiperparámetros y protocolo de fallas.\\r\\n\\r\\nMétricas principales: **MAE y RMSE en m**; skill frente a persistencia `1 - MAE_model/MAE_persistence`, con condición si el denominador es cero. Complementar con percentil 95 del error absoluto, entrenamiento/inferencia y número de sensores. R² puede acompañar; un score alto en presiones casi constantes no demuestra ganancia frente a persistencia.\\r\\n\\r\\nAnalizar por mes, hora, rango de presión y períodos de fugas usando la verdad oficial **solo después** de fijar el modelo. No asumir muestras independientes: para incertidumbre en diferencias de errores, remuestrear bloques temporales y explicar la longitud elegida; no intervalos basados en 210.240 filas iid.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Extensión opcional y riesgo\\r\\n\\r\\nEvaluar apagado de sensores auxiliares por bloques (p.ej. 15/60 min), con posiciones/máscaras fijadas y semillas publicadas. Mantener las etiquetas originales y separar datos originales de entradas perturbadas. Comparar imputación causal, máscara y ausencia de sensores; medir incremento de error frente a observación completa.\\r\\n\\r\\nIntervalos predictivos son opcionales: calibrar sin test y medir cobertura y amplitud, por mes/regímenes. La dependencia temporal y el cambio de distribución invalidan una garantía ingenua de cobertura conformal basada en intercambio iid. Presentarlos como evaluación empírica con supuestos explícitos.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Alternativas y decisión de novedad\\r\\n\\r\\n| Opción | Ventaja | Dificultad / alcance |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Forecast delta + selección por presupuesto | Coherente con Milestone 1 y con el curso | Debe demostrar ganancia y distinguirse de selección/topología existentes |\\r\\n| Forecast con fallas de sensores y máscara causal | Aporte experimental claro y operativo | Robustez ya estudiada en reconstrucción; delimitar forecast y protocolo |\\r\\n| Adaptación online tipo SAM-kNN para 15 min | Extiende kNN y estudia drift | Evitar actualizar con etiqueta antes de que transcurran 15 min; mayor complejidad |\\r\\n| Diagnóstico de fugas desde residuos | Conecta con la mayor parte de BattLeDIM | Amplía el objetivo; umbral, asociación de eventos y costo económico requieren otra evaluación |\\r\\n\\r\\nRecomendación: definir el estudio de presupuesto y forecast como eje principal, conservar fallas como extensión acotada y usar literatura de detección para motivar el problema. La novedad se establece por la **comparación experimental específica y resultados propios**, sin prometer mejora antes de medirla.\\r\\n\"}"
  }
]
```
